Actually, if the reference uses `[K//16, 2*N]`, maybe it's equivalent to `[K, N//8]` but transposed or split differently. `K/16 * 2*N = K*N/8`. And `K * N/8 = K*N/8`. A reshape might be all that's needed, but the internal ordering of the int32s matters. Actually, `[K//16, 2*N]` has `K/16` rows and `2N` columns. `[K, N//8]` has `K` rows and `N/8` columns. They have the same total elements. A reshape from `[K, N//8]` to `[K//16, 2*N]` is possible IF the elements are stored row-major: the first `2*N` elements of the K-rows version go into the first row of the K/16 version. But in memory, `[K, N//8]` row-major is equivalent to `[K//16, 16 * N//8] = [K//16, 2*N]` row-major. Yes! Because `K * N/8 = K/16 * 16 * N/8 = K/16 * 2N`. So a simple reshape makes them equivalent! The only question is the bit-packing within each int32 and the interleaving of K-rows. In the simple format, the int32 at position `(k, n//8)` packs 8 n-values for a fixed k. Within warp 0, threads 0-15 load address `addr_base + 0` (for group 0), threads 16-31 load `addr_base + 1` (for group 1). So within the warp, there are two broadcast addresses. This is still fine - the hardware can handle two broadcasts in one go or two memory transactions. So memory access pattern for B_q: - Warp 0: threads 0-15 load `B_q[k][n0/8]`, threads 16-31 load `B_q[k][n0/8+1]`. - These are adjacent addresses. Hardware might coalesce them into one or two transactions. - Actually for L1TEX, if threads in a warp access adjacent 4-byte words, they are coalesced into one 128-byte transaction. Here threads access two adjacent 4-byte words. So one or two 32-byte segments. Very efficient. Memory access for A: - Threads 0..15 (group 0) load A_smem[m][k] for different m. These are adjacent in m dimension. A_smem is probably stored as A_smem[k][m] or A_smem[m][k]. If we store as A_smem[k][m] (i.e., transpose to make m the fastest varying), then threads 0..15 read adjacent halfs = 32 bytes, perfectly coalesced in shared memory (bank conflict free if we pad or arrange properly). Actually in shared memory, it's banked, but if each thread reads a different m, and m maps to different banks (assuming 32 banks, each bank 4 bytes), then reading adjacent halfs might cause 2-way bank conflicts since each bank is 4 bytes and each thread reads 2 bytes. But with proper padding (e.g., A_smem[K_TILE][M+2]), we can avoid conflicts. Actually, we can just load A into a register array before the K loop: - The block loads A_tile[M][K_TILE] from global to shared memory. - Then each thread reads its required A values into a register: for m=threadIdx.x % 16, thread needs A[m, 0..K_TILE-1] over the loop. It can read them one by one from shared memory. Bank conflicts are minor. But we can also not use shared memory for A at all if M*K_TILE is small. 16*128*2 = 4KB. We can load A_tile into registers! Wait, each thread only needs one row of A (16 values? No, one row has K_TILE=128 values). Each row is 128 halfs = 256 bytes. There are 16 rows, total 4096 bytes. Can we fit this in registers? - 4096 bytes / 128 threads = 32 bytes per thread = 16 half registers. That's very manageable! - Actually, we can have each of the 16 threads in group 0 load one full row of A into its own registers. Then within the group, the 16 threads have all rows in registers (distributed). But all groups need all rows! Because each group computes all 16 rows for its 8 columns. That means we need to broadcast the A rows to all groups. Shared memory is the natural place for this broadcast. So A_smem it is. Let's also consider using `cp.async` to load A from global memory to shared memory asynchronously. On H100, this is beneficial. Now, dequantization logic: ```cpp // scale is shape [K/GROUP_SIZE, N] half scale = scales[kg][n_base + v]; ``` But scale is fp16. We can convert to float once: `float sf = __half2float(scale);`. Dequantized weight: `(__half)((float((w >> (v*4)) & 0xF) - 8.0f) * sf);` Accumulate: `acc[v] = __fmaf_rn(__half2float(a), w_f, acc[v]);` or use half FMA: `acc[v] = __hfma(a, w_h, acc[v]);`. For H100, half FMA is very fast. Actually, since we need high precision for correctness (atol=1e-2 or 1e-4), half accumulation over 4096 terms might have issues. The original Marlin uses fp32 reduce optionally. We should do fp32 accumulation. So: ```cpp float acc[8]; // ... float wf = (float(val) - 8.0f) * __half2float(scale); acc[v] += __half2float(a) * wf; ``` Then convert to half at the end. This avoids precision issues. Now for packing: I'll pack into `[K, N//8]` int32. Kernel design finalization: ```cpp __global__ void marlin_gemm_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const int* __restrict__ B_q, const half* __restrict__ scales, half* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int GROUP_SIZE ) { constexpr int N_TILE = 64; constexpr int K_TILE = 128; constexpr int GROUPS = N_TILE / 8; // 8 constexpr int M_DIM = 16; int n_block = blockIdx.x; int n_start = n_block * N_TILE; int g = threadIdx.x / M_DIM; int m = threadIdx.x % M_DIM; if (m >= M) return; // M might be <= 16 float acc[8]; #pragma unroll for (int v = 0; v < 8; v++) acc[v] = 0.0f; // Shared memory for A tile [K_TILE][M_DIM] (padded) __shared__ half A_smem[K_TILE][M_DIM + 2]; // Shared memory for scales [GROUPS][8] (one scale per column) __shared__ half scales_smem[GROUPS][8]; for (int kk = 0; kk < K; kk += K_TILE) { // Load A tile into shared memory // A is [M, K] row-major // Each thread loads multiple elements for (int t = threadIdx.x; t < K_TILE * M_DIM; t += blockDim.x) { int k_local = t / M_DIM; int mm = t % M_DIM; int global_k = kk + k_local; half val = (global_k < K && mm < M) ? A[mm * K + global_k] : __float2half(0.0f); A_smem[k_local][mm] = val; } // Load scales for this K group (same for all k in tile if K_TILE <= GROUP_SIZE) // But GROUP_SIZE=128 and K_TILE=128, so one scale group per tile int kg = kk / GROUP_SIZE; for (int t = threadIdx.x; t < GROUPS * 8; t += blockDim.x) { int gg = t / 8; int vc = t % 8; int col = n_start + gg * 8 + vc; scales_smem[gg][vc] = (kg < K / GROUP_SIZE && col < N) ? scales[kg * N + col] : __float2half(0.0f); } __syncthreads(); // Each thread processes its group of 8 columns and one row m int col_group = g; int col_base = col_group * 8; #pragma unroll 8 for (int k_local = 0; k_local < K_TILE; k_local++) { int global_k = kk + k_local; if (global_k >= K) break; // Load B_q for this k and this column group // B_q shape [K, N/8] int b_idx = global_k * (N / 8) + (n_start + col_base) / 8; int w_int = B_q[b_idx]; half a_val = A_smem[k_local][m]; float a_f = __half2float(a_val); #pragma unroll for (int v = 0; v < 8; v++) { int val4 = (w_int >> (v * 4)) & 0xF; float w_f = (float(val4) - 8.0f) * __half2float(scales_smem[col_group][v]); acc[v] = fmaf(a_f, w_f, acc[v]); } } __syncthreads(); } // Store results #pragma unroll for (int v = 0; v < 8; v++) { int col = n_start + g * 8 + v; if (col < N && m < M) { C[m * N + col] = __float2half(acc[v]); } } } ``` This kernel has some issues: 1. `N/8` integer division in the inner loop: `global_k * (N / 8)`. This is constant for all threads in a block for a given k, but changes with k. We can precompute `n_word_offset = (n_start + col_base) / 8` and then `b_idx = global_k * (N/8) + n_word_offset`. Actually `global_k * (N/8)` is the row offset. We can maintain a running pointer: `b_row_ptr = B_q + kk*(N/8) + ...`. In the loop, increment by `N/8` each iteration. Better! - `const int* b_row = B_q + (n_start + col_base) / 8;` - In loop: `int w_int = b_row[global_k * (N/8)]`? No, if `B_q` is `[K][N/8]`, then element is `B_q[global_k * (N/8) + word_idx]`. - We can set `const int* b_ptr = B_q + word_idx;` then in loop `int w_int = b_ptr[global_k * (N/8)];`. - To avoid multiplication: `b_ptr += (N / 8);` at each k step? Then it's a dependent pointer increment. Or maintain `int row_stride = N / 8;` and compute `b_ptr[row_stride * k_local]`. Since K_TILE=128 is constant, the compiler might unroll and strength-reduce. 2. The scale is loaded from shared memory each iteration. If `M_DIM+2` padding, and since threads 0..15 have m = tid%16, they are in different groups? No, threads in group 0 have m=0..15. They access A_smem[k][0], A_smem[k][1], ..., A_smem[k][15] simultaneously. These are adjacent halfs. With 32 banks of 4 bytes, 2 halfs per bank. So thread m reads bank `m/2`. Threads 0,1 both access bank 0 (since address = base + k*(M_DIM+2)*2 + m*2; bank = (m/2) % 32). So there is a 2-way bank conflict for every pair of adjacent threads. That's acceptable. Since PyTorch tensors are row-major, a reshape from `(K, N//8)` to `(K//16, N*2)` is just a view if `K*(N//8) == (K//16)*(N*2)` -> `K*N/8 == K*N/8*16/16` wait: - `K * (N/8)` vs `(K/16) * (2N) = K*N/8`. Yes, same total elements. - But are they contiguous in the same order? - `(K, N/8)` row-major: element `(k, w)` is at offset `k*(N/8) + w`. - `(K/16, 2N)` row-major: element `(kr, wc)` is at offset `kr*(2N) + wc`. - If we reshape with `view(K//16, 2*N)`, the first `2N` elements of the first row correspond to `k=0` and all `w=0..2N-1`. But in the `(K, N/8)` layout, row 0 has `N/8` elements. Row 1 has next `N/8`. So after `N/8` elements, we move to k=1. But in reshaped `(K/16, 2N)`, after `2N` elements we move to the next kr. - `2N` vs `N/8`: these are different unless N=16. So a simple reshape does NOT preserve element order! The memory layouts are different. - Example: K=32, N=64. Simple shape: (32, 8). Marlin shape: (2, 128). - Simple: row 0 has 8 elements. Row 1 has next 8. Total 16 elements for first marlin row (128 elements). Marlin shape row 0 expects 128 consecutive elements from simple layout. That's 16 rows of simple layout. - Indeed: K=32, K/16=2. 16 rows of simple layout fit into one row of marlin layout. So a reshape from (K, N/8) to (K/16, 16 * N/8) = (K/16, 2N) is just grouping 16 simple rows into one marlin row. It IS a valid view/reshape if we first reshape to `(K//16, 16, N//8)` and then flatten the last two dims to `(K//16, 16*N//8) = (K//16, 2N)`. This is a valid view! - How? `qweight.view(K//16, 16, N//8).permute(0, 2, 1).contiguous().view(K//16, 2*N)`? No, if we just reshape without permutation: `(K, N//8)` -> `(K//16, 16 * N//8) = (K//16, 2N)`. This is a valid reshape if the tensor is contiguous: it simply groups every 16 rows into one longer row. The memory ordering remains the same! The first `2N` elements are exactly the first 16 rows concatenated. - Yes! So `qweight.view(K//16, 2*N)` is a valid view of the `(K, N//8)` data. And the memory layout is: for each block of 16 K-rows, all their int32 words are stored consecutively. So if I generate the simple `(K, N//8)` layout and then do `.view(K//16, 2*N)`, the underlying memory has the simple row-major order where `offset = k * (N/8) + word_idx`. I'll implement quantization: ```python class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, K, N, GROUP_SIZE): super().__init__() self.K = K self.N = N self.GROUP_SIZE = GROUP_SIZE torch.manual_seed(0) weight = torch.randn(K, N, dtype=torch.float16, device='cuda') # Symmetric per-group quantization to unsigned 4-bit with bias 8 num_groups = K // GROUP_SIZE w_reshaped = weight.view(num_groups, GROUP_SIZE, N) maxabs = w_reshaped.abs().amax(dim=1, keepdim=True) # [num_groups, 1, N] scale = (maxabs / 7.0).clamp(min=1e-6) # avoid div by zero # Quantize w_int = torch.clamp(torch.round(w_reshaped / scale) + 8, 0, 15).to(torch.uint8) # Pack into int32: 8 uint4 per int32 # w_int shape [num_groups, GROUP_SIZE, N], values 0-15 w_int = w_int.view(K, N) # back to 2D # Pack 8 consecutive N values into one int32 # Need to ensure N is divisible by 8 assert N % 8 == 0 w_int = w_int.view(K, N // 8, 8) # [K, N/8, 8] # Shift and OR shifts = torch.arange(0, 32, 4, dtype=torch.int32, device='cuda').view(1, 1, 8) b_q_weight = (w_int.to(torch.int32) << shifts).sum(dim=-1) # [K, N/8] # Reshape to Marlin-compatible shape [K//16, 2*N] b_q_weight = b_q_weight.view(K // 16, 2 * N) scale = scale.squeeze(1) # [num_groups, N] self.register_buffer('b_q_weight', b_q_weight) self.register_buffer('b_scales', scale.half()) def forward(self, x): # x: [M, K] M = x.size(0) # Call custom kernel c = marlin_gemm_h100(x, self.b_q_weight, self.b_scales, self.GROUP_SIZE) return c ``` Wait, the `scale / 7.0` logic: `maxabs / 7.0` means scale = maxabs / 7. Then the max quantized signed value is 7, unsigned is 15. `w_true = (u - 8) * scale`. `max_true = (15-8)*scale = 7*scale = maxabs`. `min_true = (0-8)*scale = -8*scale = -8/7*maxabs`. This clips values in [-maxabs, -8/7*maxabs] slightly if they existed. But since maxabs is symmetric, the negative range is actually [-maxabs, maxabs], and we clip everything < -7*scale to -8*scale (a bit more negative). This is standard for symmetric int4 if you use scale = maxabs / 7.0. Some use scale = maxabs / 8.0 to make [-8,7] fit, which would map maxabs to 8*scale -> 8, stored as 16 -> overflow. So maxabs/7.0 is correct. Actually, for unsigned with zero-point, the common formula is: - Find `wmin`, `wmax`. - `scale = (wmax - wmin) / 15.0` - `zero = round(-wmin / scale)` But for symmetric, `wmin = -maxabs`, `wmax = maxabs`. - `scale = (2*maxabs) / 15.0` - `zero = round(maxabs / scale) = 7.5 -> 8` approximately. Then quantized values: `(w / scale + 8)`. Range maps: `maxabs -> 7.5+8 = 15.5 -> clamp 15`. `-maxabs -> -7.5+8 = 0.5 -> 1` or `0` depending on rounding. This uses the full range but includes zero point. For symmetric with fixed zero-point 8: - `scale = maxabs / 7.0` - `u = clamp(round(w / scale) + 8, 0, 15)` This seems reasonable. I'll use this. Let me write out the complete kernel more carefully. ```cpp __global__ void marlin_gemm_h100_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const int* __restrict__ B_q, const half* __restrict__ scales, half* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int GROUP_SIZE ) { constexpr int N_TILE = 64; constexpr int K_TILE = 128; constexpr int M_DIM = 16; int n_block = blockIdx.x; int n_start = n_block * N_TILE; int tid = threadIdx.x; int g = tid / M_DIM; // column group 0..7 int m = tid % M_DIM; // row 0..15 if (m >= M || n_start >= N) return; float acc[8]; #pragma unroll for (int v = 0; v < 8; v++) acc[v] = 0.0f; __shared__ alignas(16) half A_smem[M_DIM][K_TILE + 2]; __shared__ half scales_smem[N_TILE / 8][8]; const int n_words = N / 8; for (int kk = 0; kk < K; kk += K_TILE) { // Load A tile: [M][K_TILE] // A is row-major: A[m][k] = A[m * K + k] #pragma unroll for (int t = tid; t < M_DIM * K_TILE; t += blockDim.x) { int mm = t / K_TILE; int k_local = t % K_TILE; int gk = kk + k_local; half val = (mm < M && gk < K) ? A[mm * K + gk] : __float2half(0.0f); A_smem[mm][k_local] = val; } // Load scales for this K group int kg = kk / GROUP_SIZE; #pragma unroll for (int t = tid; t < (N_TILE / 8) * 8; t += blockDim.x) { int gg = t / 8; int vc = t % 8; int col = n_start + gg * 8 + vc; scales_smem[gg][vc] = (kg < K / GROUP_SIZE && col < N) ? scales[kg * N + col] : __float2half(0.0f); } __syncthreads(); float scale_reg[8]; #pragma unroll for (int v = 0; v < 8; v++) { scale_reg[v] = __half2float(scales_smem[g][v]); } int col_word_offset = (n_start + g * 8) / 8; const int* b_ptr = B_q + col_word_offset; #pragma unroll 16 for (int k_local = 0; k_local < K_TILE; k_local++) { int gk = kk + k_local; if (gk >= K) break; int w_int = b_ptr[gk * n_words]; half a_val = A_smem[m][k_local]; float a_f = __half2float(a_val); #pragma unroll for (int v = 0; v < 8; v++) { int val4 = (w_int >> (v * 4)) & 0xF; float w_f = (float(val4) - 8.0f) * scale_reg[v]; acc[v] = fmaf(a_f, w_f, acc[v]); } } __syncthreads(); } int col_base = n_start + g * 8; #pragma unroll for (int v = 0; v < 8; v++) { int col = col_base + v; if (col < N && m < M) { C[m * N + col] = __float2half(acc[v]); } } } ``` Wait, `blockDim.x = 128`. The loop `for (int t = tid; t < M_DIM * K_TILE; t += blockDim.x)` runs `2048/128 = 16` iterations. With `#pragma unroll`, the compiler might not unroll 16 iterations. I should not force unroll on this loop. Actually 16 iterations is fine to unroll but increases code size. It's okay. `gk * n_words` in the inner loop: `n_words = N/8 = 512`. This multiplication can be strength-reduced: `int b_idx = gk * n_words + col_word_offset;` and increment by `n_words` each iteration. Since K_TILE=128 and we `pragma unroll 16`, the compiler might not fully unroll 128 iterations. `#pragma unroll 16` means unroll by factor 16, not fully. Or `pragma unroll` without number might fully unroll depending on compiler heuristics. Let's strength-reduce manually: ```cpp const int* b_row_ptr = B_q + kk * n_words + col_word_offset; for (int k_local = 0; k_local < K_TILE; k_local++) { int w_int = *b_row_ptr; b_row_ptr += n_words; // ... } ``` This avoids multiplication in the inner loop. A[mm * K + gk] : __float2half(0.0f); A_smem[mm][k_local] = val; } int kg = kk / group_size; for (int t = tid; t < (N_TILE / 8) * 8; t += blockDim.x) { int gg = t / 8; int vc = t % 8; int col = n_start + gg * 8 + vc; int w_int = b_row_ptr[gk * n_words]; half a_val = A_smem[m][k_local]; float a_f = __half2float(a_val); #pragma unroll for (int v = 0; v < 8; v++) { int val4 = (w_int >> (v * 4)) & 0xF; float w_f = (float(val4) - 8.0f) * scale_reg[v]; } } } torch::Tensor marlin_gemm_h100( torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B_q, torch::Tensor scales, int64_t group_size ) { int M = A.size(0); int K = A.size(1); int N = scales.size(1); TORCH_CHECK(N % 64 == 0, "N must be divisible by 64"); For consecutive k values (k and k+1), the addresses differ by `n_words * 4 = 2048 bytes`. This is larger than a cache line, so each K step loads a new cache line into L1/L2. L2 can handle this streaming pattern. To improve locality, we could transpose B_q to `[N/8, K]` so that consecutive K for a fixed group are adjacent in memory. But the standard layout in Marlin might be `[K, N/8]`. If I control the layout, I can choose `B_q` layout as `[N/8, K]`! That would make the inner loop read consecutive memory: `b_ptr[k_local]` without stride. This would be much more cache friendly! Let's do that! In `__init__`, instead of packing as `[K, N//8]`, I can pack as `[N//8, K]` (column-major in effect). But the buffer shape exposed is `[K//16, 2*N]`. The underlying memory can be viewed/transposed. Actually, let's store `b_q_weight` as `[N//8, K]` and in the kernel access `B_q[col_word_offset * K + gk]`. This means consecutive `gk` are adjacent. The inner loop becomes: ```cpp const int* b_col_ptr = B_q + col_word_offset * K + kk; for (int k_local = 0; k_local < K_TILE; k_local++) { int w_int = b_col_ptr[k_local]; ... } ``` This is much better for cache! The entire K=4096 column group is stored contiguously (4KB). When we iterate over K_TILE=128 of it, we get perfect sequential access. Adjacent threads in the warp read adjacent `col_word_offset`, so at fixed k_local, they read adjacent column groups. These are adjacent in memory if `col_word_offset` differs by 1: `offset1 = (col_word_offset+1)*K + gk`. Since K=4096, this is 4096*4 = 16384 bytes away! That's not adjacent. So within a warp, threads reading different column groups are far apart. But each thread reads sequentially down its own column group. This is the "column-wise" access pattern. In GPUs, threads in a warp should ideally access adjacent addresses for coalescing. Here, thread in group 0 accesses `col0*K + gk`, thread in group 1 accesses `col1*K + gk`. The difference is K*4 = 16384 bytes. These are definitely not coalesced. In fact, they fall into different cache lines and possibly different memory banks. This is TERRIBLE for memory coalescing. So row-major `[K, N/8]` is actually better for coalescing within a warp at fixed K! Because threads in the same warp access adjacent column groups in the same row. This is coalesced! The strided access across K is the trade-off. Actually, for matrix B in GEMM, the access pattern is usually along K. Since it's row-major `[K, N/8]`, within a warp, threads access adjacent columns (adjacent words), which is coalesced. Across K, it's strided by `N/8`. This is similar to standard GEMM where you access B[k, n] and iterate k. GPUs handle this via L2 cache. Can we improve K-locality while keeping coalescing? We can store B_q in blocks/tiles: `[K/TILE, N/8, TILE]` where the innermost dimension is K. So for a fixed column group and tile, K values are consecutive. And within a warp, different threads access different column groups within the same K tile. This is exactly the standard blocked matrix layout! Specifically, a tile layout: `B_q_tile[K_TILE][N_WORDS_TILE]` where `N_WORDS_TILE` is for the block's N range. But since different blocks handle different N ranges, we can store the weight matrix in `[N/8, K]`? No, that's transpose. Actually, the standard coalesced GEMM pattern for loading B as [K, N] is: - Threads in a warp access adjacent N for a fixed K (coalesced). - Then K increments, accessing adjacent N on the next row. This is exactly what row-major `[K, N/8]` gives us! The issue is that consecutive K accesses are strided by `N/8` in memory, which might hurt cache. But L2 cache prefetchers often handle strided access. Also, on H100, L2 is large enough to capture some reuse. If we tile K (e.g., K_TILE=128), then for each tile, we read 128 rows spaced by `N/8`. On H100, L2 cache lines are 128 bytes. Each row read fetches a line containing 32 column groups. Our block uses 8 column groups. So each memory transaction brings 32 groups. This is good because other warps or SMs might need other column groups, but more importantly, the L2 cache line contains data for the same K and neighboring column groups. Actually, consecutive K rows with the same N range: row k loads addresses `k*N_WORDS + base`, row k+1 loads `(k+1)*N_WORDS + base`. Difference = N_WORDS*4 = 2048 bytes. This is exactly 16 cache lines (128 bytes each). So each K step loads a new cache line. No reuse. But within the K loop for a thread, 128 iterations access 128 different cache lines. If we have many blocks, L2 might not hold them all. But with 64 blocks and L2=50MB, B_q total is 8MB, scales 256KB, A 128KB. L2 can easily hold everything! In fact, it can hold the entire B_q matrix. So after the first few blocks load B_q into L2, all subsequent accesses are L2 cache hits! This means bandwidth is effectively L2 bandwidth, which is huge. So the simple row-major layout is fine, even excellent. Okay, I'll keep `[K, N/8]` layout. One optimization: since L2 can hold the entire B_q (8MB), we actually want to maximize cache hit rate by having blocks share the same K data. In our kernel, different blocks (different N) load the same K rows but different column groups. The column groups for different N blocks are far apart in memory. But L2 is 50MB, so plenty of room. assistant```python import torch import torch.nn as nn from torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline _CUDA_SOURCE = r""" #include <torch/extension.h> #include <cuda_fp16.h> #include <cuda_runtime.h> __global__ void marlin_gemm_h100_kernel( const half* __restrict__ A, const int* __restrict__ B_q, const half* __restrict__ scales, half* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int group_size ) { constexpr int N_TILE = 64; constexpr int K_TILE = 128; constexpr int M_DIM = 16; int n_block = blockIdx.x; int n_start = n_block * N_TILE; int tid = threadIdx.x; int g = tid / M_DIM; // column int32 group 0..7 int m = tid % M_DIM; // row 0..15 float acc[8]; #pragma unroll for (int v = 0; v < 8; v++) acc[v] = 0.0f; __shared__ alignas(16) half A_smem[M_DIM][K_TILE + 2]; __shared__ half scales_smem[N_TILE / 8][8]; const int n_words = N / 8; for (int kk = 0; kk < K; kk += K_TILE) { // Cooperative load of A tile: [M_DIM][K_TILE] for (int t = tid; t < M_DIM * K_TILE; t += blockDim.x) { int mm = t / K_TILE; int k_local = t % K_TILE; int gk = kk + k_local; half val = (mm < M && gk < K) ? A[mm * K + gk] : __float2half(0.0f); A_smem[mm][k_local] = val; } // Cooperative load of scales for this K group int kg = kk / group_size; for (int t = tid; t < (N_TILE / 8) * 8; t += blockDim.x) { int gg = t / 8; int vc = t % 8; int col = n_start + gg * 8 + vc; scales_smem[gg][vc] = (col < N) ? scales[kg * N + col] : __float2half(0.0f); } __syncthreads(); if (m < M) { float scale_reg[8]; #pragma unroll for (int v = 0; v < 8; v++) { scale_reg[v] = __half2float(scales_smem[g][v]); } int col_word_offset = (n_start + g * 8) / 8; const int* b_iter = B_q + kk * n_words + col_word_offset; #pragma unroll 16 for (int k_local = 0; k_local < K_TILE; k_local++) { int gk = kk + k_local; if (gk >= K) break; int w_int = *b_iter; b_iter += n_words; half a_val = A_smem[m][k_local]; float a_f = __half2float(a_val); #pragma unroll for (int v = 0; v < 8; v++) { int val4 = (w_int >> (v * 4)) & 0xF; float w_f = (float(val4) - 8.0f) * scale_reg[v]; acc[v] = fmaf(a_f, w_f, acc[v]); } } } __syncthreads(); } if (m < M) { int col_base = n_start + g * 8; #pragma unroll for (int v = 0; v < 8; v++) { int col = col_base + v; if (col < N) { C[m * N + col] = __float2half(acc[v]); } } } } torch::Tensor marlin_gemm_h100( torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B_q, torch::Tensor scales, int64_t group_size ) { int M = A.size(0); int K = A.size(1); int N = scales.size(1); TORCH_CHECK(A.is_cuda(), "A must be CUDA"); TORCH_CHECK(B_q.is_cuda(), "B_q must be CUDA"); TORCH_CHECK(scales.is_cuda(), "scales must be CUDA"); TORCH_CHECK(A.dtype() == torch::kHalf, "A must be fp16"); TORCH_CHECK(B_q.dtype() == torch::kInt32 || B_q.dtype() == torch::kInt, "B_q must be int32"); TORCH_CHECK(scales.dtype() == torch::kHalf, "scales must be fp16"); TORCH_CHECK(A.is_contiguous(), "A must be contiguous"); TORCH_CHECK(B_q.is_contiguous(), "B_q must be contiguous"); TORCH_CHECK(scales.is_contiguous(), "scales must be contiguous"); TORCH_CHECK(N % 64 == 0, "N must be divisible by 64"); TORCH_CHECK(K % 128 == 0, "K must be divisible by 128"); auto C = torch::empty({M, N}, A.options()); dim3 blocks(N / 64); dim3 threads(128); marlin_gemm_h100_kernel<<<blocks, threads>>>( reinterpret_cast<const half*>(A.data_ptr<at::Half>()), B_q.data_ptr<int>(), reinterpret_cast<const half*>(scales.data_ptr<at::Half>()), reinterpret_cast<half*>(C.data_ptr<at::Half>()), M, N, K, static_cast<int>(group_size) ); return C; } """ _CPP_SOURCE = r""" #include <torch/extension.h> torch::Tensor marlin_gemm_h100(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B_q, torch::Tensor scales, int64_t group_size); PYBIND11_MODULE(TORCH_EXTENSION_NAME, m) { m.def("marlin_gemm_h100", &marlin_gemm_h100, "Marlin GEMM H100"); } """ _marlin_gemm_h100 = load_inline( name="marlin_gemm_h100", cpp_sources=_CPP_SOURCE, cuda_sources=_CUDA_SOURCE, functions=["marlin_gemm_h100"], extra_cuda_cflags=["-O3", "--use_fast_math"], extra_cflags=["-O3"], verbose=False, ) class ModelNew(nn.Module): def __init__(self, K, N, GROUP_SIZE): super().__init__() self.K = K self.N = N self.GROUP_SIZE = GROUP_SIZE torch.manual_seed(0) weight = torch.randn(K, N, dtype=torch.float16, device="cuda") num_groups = K // GROUP_SIZE w_reshaped = weight.view(num_groups, GROUP_SIZE, N) maxabs = w_reshaped.abs().amax(dim=1, keepdim=True) scale = (maxabs / 7.0).clamp(min=1e-6) w_int = torch.clamp(torch.round(w_reshaped / scale) + 8, 0, 15).to(torch.uint8) w_int = w_int.view(K, N) shifts = torch.arange(0, 32, 4, dtype=torch.int32, device="cuda") w_packed = (w_int.to(torch.int32).unsqueeze(-1) << shifts).sum(dim=-1) b_q_weight = w_packed.view(K // 16, 2 * N) scale = scale.squeeze(1) self.register_buffer("b_q_weight", b_q_weight.contiguous()) self.register_buffer("b_scales", scale.half().contiguous()) def forward(self, x): return _marlin_gemm_h100(x, self.b_q_weight, self.b_scales, self.GROUP_SIZE) ```

Let me write the kernel: ```cuda __global__ void marlin_w4a16_gemm_h100( const half* __restrict__ A, const int* __restrict__ B_q, const half* __restrict__ scales, half* __restrict__ C, int M, int N, int K, int group_size ) { constexpr int N_TILE = 128; constexpr int K_TILE = 256; int n_block = blockIdx.x; int n_start = n_block * N_TILE; int tid = threadIdx.x; int lane = tid % 32; int warp = tid / 32; __shared__ alignas(128) half A_smem[16][K_TILE]; __shared__ alignas(128) half scales_smem[N_TILE]; // Each warp handles a portion of M and N // 8 warps, let's assign: // Warp 0-3: handle rows 0-15, N groups 0-3 (32 elements each) // Actually, let's do: each thread handles M elements across K_TILE // Simpler: each thread computes a subset of output elements // 256 threads, output is M x N_TILE = 16 x 128 = 2048 elements // Each thread handles 2048 / 256 = 8 output elements int out_per_thread = 8; int thread_out_start = tid * out_per_thread; int m_idx = thread_out_start / N_TILE; int n_idx = thread_out_start % N_TILE; // Actually, let's structure differently for better memory access // Use vectorized loads and assign threads to M x N tiles float acc[8] = {0}; for (int kk = 0; kk < K; kk += K_TILE) { // Load A into smem using all threads // A is M x K, load M x K_TILE for (int t = tid; t < M * K_TILE; t += blockDim.x) { int mm = t / K_TILE; int k_local = t % K_TILE; int gk = kk + k_local; A_smem[mm][k_local] = (gk < K) ? __ldg(&scales[kg * N + col]) : __float2half(0.0f); } __syncthreads(); // Each thread computes 8 outputs (1 M row, 8 N columns) if (m_idx < M && n_idx < N_TILE) { int m = m_idx; int n_base = n_idx; #pragma unroll for (int k_local = 0; k_local < K_TILE; k_local++) { half a_val = A_smem[m][k_local]; float a_f = __half2float(a_val); int global_k = kk + k_local; if (global_k >= K) break; // Load 8 consecutive N weights int word_idx = global_k * (N / 8) + (n_start + n_base) / 8; int w_int = __ldg(&B_q[word_idx]); #pragma unroll for (int v = 0; v < 8; v++) { int w4 = (w_int >> (v * 4)) & 0xF; float w_f = (float(w4) - 8.0f) * __half2float(scales_smem[n_base + v]); acc[v] = fmaf(a_f, w_f, acc[v]); } } } __syncthreads(); } // Write results if (m_idx < M && n_idx < N_TILE) { int m = m_idx; #pragma unroll for (int v = 0; v < 8; v++) { int col = n_start + n_idx + v; if (col < N) { C[m * N + col] = __float2half(acc[v]); } } } } ``` Hmm, I think there are issues with this mapping. Actually, let me try to figure out what's optimal. On H100, shared memory bandwidth is very high (~~30 TB/s or so). So we want to use shared memory for A and scales. In my kernel, B is accessed as: ```cpp int w_int = B_q[gk * n_words + (n_start + n_base) / 8]; ``` For consecutive threads in a warp, `n_base` differs but `gk` is the same. So threads in a warp read different B addresses (strided by n_words / 8 words). This is NOT coalesced! To coalesce, we want consecutive threads to read consecutive memory addresses. So threads should have the same `n_base` (or consecutive `n_base`) and different `gk`. Currently, threads are arranged as: - tid 0: m=0, n_base=0 - tid 1: m=0, n_base=4 - tid 2: m=0, n_base=8 - ... If n_base=0,4,8,12,... then (n_start + n_base) / 8 = n_start/8, n_start/8 + 0, n_start/8 + 1, etc. (if each n_base covers 4 columns, then each int32 covers 8 columns, so n_base=0,4 would be the same int32 if n_base is in terms of columns... wait) I defined n_base as the starting column for each thread. If each thread handles 4 columns, and each int32 packs 8 columns, then: - Thread handles cols n_base, n_base+1, n_base+2, n_base+3 - But int32 packs 8 cols. So if n_base=0, the int32 covers cols 0-7. These are in int32 word 0 (covers cols 0-7). - For thread with n_base=4: it needs cols 4,5,6,7. These are also in int32 word 0. - So multiple threads need the same int32! If warp has threads for 4 rows, each with 8 different column groups... - Consecutive thread IDs might not correspond to consecutive memory addresses. Let me define the thread assignment carefully: ```cuda int compute_tid = tid % 128; // Only 128 threads do compute int m = compute_tid / 8; // 0..15 (but only 0..7 active if M_TILE=8) int n_group = compute_tid % 8; // 0..7 int n_base = n_group * 8; ``` Hmm wait, with M_TILE=8, m ranges from 0..7. So compute_tid / 8 gives m=0..15, but we only need m=0..7. Let me use: ```cuda int m = (compute_tid / 8) % M_TILE; ``` Or simpler, with M_TILE=8 and threads_per_row=8: ```cuda int m = compute_tid / 8; // 0..15 if (m >= M_TILE) m -= M_TILE; // Wrap around? No that's weird. // Better: int m = compute_tid % M_TILE; int n_group = compute_tid / M_TILE; ``` With M_TILE=8: - compute_tid 0..7: m=0..7, n_group=0 - compute_tid 8..15: m=0..7, n_group=1 - ... - compute_tid 120..127: m=0..7, n_group=15? But N_TILE=64 only has 8 groups. Wait, N_TILE=64 / 8 = 8 groups. So n_group should be 0..7. compute_threads = M_TILE * (N_TILE / 8) = 8 * 8 = 128. So: ```cuda int m = compute_tid % M_TILE; // 0..7 int n_group = compute_tid / M_TILE; // 0..7 int n_base = n_group * 8; ``` For consecutive compute_tid: - 0: m=0, n=0 - 1: m=1, n=0 - ... - 7: m=7, n=0 - 8: m=0, n=8 - 9: m=1, n=8 So threads with the same n_group but different m read the SAME B address. This is NOT coalesced within a warp! A warp has 32 threads. With this assignment, threads 0-7 read B at n=0, threads 8-15 read B at n=8, etc. So 4 threads in a warp read the same B address (for n=0, 8, 16, 24). This is bad for memory bandwidth. To coalesce B reads, we want threads in a warp to read consecutive B addresses. Consecutive B addresses correspond to consecutive n_group values. But with n_group changing every M_TILE threads, threads in a warp have different n_groups only if M_TILE >= 32 or if we reorder. Actually, with M_TILE=8, 32 threads in a warp would have: - Threads 0-7: n_group=0 (B at n=0) - Threads 8-15: n_group=1 (B at n=8) - Threads 16-23: n_group=2 (B at n=16) - Threads 24-31: n_group=3 (B at n=24) So within a warp, 4 different B addresses are read, each by 8 threads. This means 8 threads do broadcast reads (same address). On H100, broadcast reads are efficient, but we're only utilizing 4 cache lines per warp instead of 16+ for fully coalesced. To improve, we could transpose the assignment: ```cuda int m = compute_tid / (N_TILE / 8); // 0..15 (but capped to M_TILE) int n_group = compute_tid % (N_TILE / 8); // 0..7 ``` Then consecutive threads have consecutive n_group: - Thread 0: m=0, n=0 - Thread 1: m=0, n=8 - Thread 2: m=0, n=16 - ... - Thread 7: m=0, n=56 - Thread 8: m=1, n=0 - Thread 9: m=1, n=8 In a warp (32 threads): - Threads 0-7: m=0, n=0,8,16,24,32,40,48,56 - Threads 8-15: m=1, n=0,8,16,24,32,40,48,56 - Threads 16-23: m=2, n=0,8,16,24,32,40,48,56 - Threads 24-31: m=3, n=0,8,16,24,32,40,48,56 Within a warp, threads read B at n=0,8,16,24,32,40,48,56 (8 addresses), each read by 4 threads. This is better but still not ideal. Actually, the ideal pattern for B would be: all threads in a warp read the same `gk` and consecutive `n` addresses. For H100 with 3.35 TB/s bandwidth and 80GB memory, the main bottleneck for small M is likely memory bandwidth (reading A, B_q, scales). For M=16, N=4096, K=4096: - Reading A: 16 * 4096 * 2 bytes = 131072 bytes - Reading B_q: 4096/16 * 2 * 4096 * 4 bytes = 256 * 8192 = 2097152 bytes... wait Actually B_q is [K/16, 2*N] = [256, 8192] of int32: 256 * 8192 * 4 = 8388608 bytes - Reading scales: 4096/128 * 4096 * 2 bytes = 32 * 4096 * 2 = 262144 bytes - Writing C: 16 * 4096 * 2 = 131072 bytes Total memory traffic: ~8.9 MB At 3.35 TB/s, theoretical minimum time is ~2.65 microseconds. For compute: each output is a dot product of 4096 elements. 16 * 4096 = 65536 dot products, each with 4096 MACs. Total MACs = 65536 * 4096 = 268.4 million. At 1979 TFLOPS FP16, this is ~0.136 microseconds. So compute is not the bottleneck. For my kernel, the main concern is memory bandwidth utilization. For good utilization: 1. Coalesced global memory access 2. Shared memory for reducing redundant loads 3. Sufficient parallelism With my design of cooperative loading into smem, I think I can achieve good bandwidth utilization. OK, let me just write the code. I'll make the kernel as clean and correct as possible. Wait, one more thought. M=16, N=4096 is a GEMV-like operation (small M, large N and K). For GEMV, the optimal strategy is different from GEMM. Typically: - Each block handles a range of N outputs - Threads within a block cooperate to load and share A (which is small: 16 * K * 2 bytes) - Each thread computes its assigned N outputs by iterating over K For M=16, A is only 128KB (for K=4096). Actually, 16 * 4096 * 2 = 131072 bytes. This fits in shared memory! In fact, it fits in L2 cache easily. So a simpler approach: 1. Load all of A into shared memory (or just access from global - it'll be cached in L2) 2. Each block handles N_TILE columns 3. For each K tile, load B_q weights for those N_TILE columns 4. Compute dot products Actually, for even simpler code, I can just have each thread load its own weights and compute. A will be in L2 cache due to reuse. But for correctness and matching the reference, let me just write the cooperative approach. I can optimize later if needed. Let me write the code now. I'll keep the kernel relatively simple but correct. For H100-specific optimizations: 1. Use `--use_fast_math` 2. Target sm_90 3. Use larger shared memory (228KB max per block) if needed 4. Use enough threads for good occupancy For our kernel, with ~37KB shared memory and 256 threads, we can fit multiple blocks per SM. OK, let me just write the code. No more deliberation! callcompile_kernel { "kernel_code": "\nimport torch\nimport torch.nn as nn\nfrom torch.utils.cpp_extension import load_inline\n\ncpp_source = r'''\n#include <torch/extension.h>\ntorch::Tensor w4a16_gemm_h100(torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B_q, torch::Tensor scales, int64_t group_size);\n'''\n\ncuda_source = r'''\n#include <torch/extension.h>\n#include <cuda_fp16.h>\n#include <cuda_runtime.h>\n#include <stdint.h>\n\n__global__ void w4a16_gemm_h100_kernel(\n const half* __restrict__ A,\n const int* __restrict__ B_q,\n const half* __restrict__ scales,\n half* __restrict__ C,\n int M, int N, int K, int group_size\n) {\n constexpr int N_TILE = 128;\n constexpr int K_TILE = 128;\n \n int n_block = blockIdx.x;\n int n_start = n_block * N_TILE;\n int tid = threadIdx.x;\n \n int m = tid / 16;\n int n_sub = tid % 16;\n int n_thread_start = n_sub * 8;\n \n __shared__ alignas(128) half A_smem[16][K_TILE];\n __shared__ alignas(128) half B_dq_smem[K_TILE][N_TILE];\n __shared__ alignas(128) half scale_smem[N_TILE];\n \n float acc[8];\n #pragma unroll\n for (int v = 0; v < 8; v++) acc[v] = 0.0f;\n \n for (int kk = 0; kk < K; kk += K_TILE) {\n // Load A tile\n for (int t = tid; t < M * K_TILE; t += blockDim.x) {\n int row = t / K_TILE;\n int col = t % K_TILE;\n A_smem[row][col] = A[row * K + kk + col];\n }\n \n // Load scales for this K group\n int scale_g = kk / group_size;\n for (int t = tid; t < N_TILE; t += blockDim.x) {\n scale_smem[t] = scales[scale_g * N + n_start + t];\n }\n \n __syncthreads();\n \n // Load and dequantize B weights\n for (int t = tid; t < K_TILE * N_TILE; t += blockDim.x) {\n int k_local = t / N_TILE;\n int n_local = t % N_TILE;\n int k_global = kk + k_local;\n int n = n_start + n_local;\n \n int k_s = k_global / 16;\n int k_in = k_global % 16;\n int k_int = k_in / 8;\n int bit_idx = k_in % 8;\n \n int w_int = B_q[k_s * (2 * N) + n * 2 + k_int];\n int w4 = (w_int >> (bit_idx * 4)) & 0xF;\n \n half sc = scale_smem[n_local];\n float w_f = (float(w4) - 8.0f) * __half2float(sc);\n B_dq_smem[k_local][n_local] = __float2half(w_f);\n }\n \n __syncthreads();\n \n // Compute\n if (m < M) {\n #pragma unroll 8\n for (int k_local = 0; k_local < K_TILE; k_local++) {\n half a_val = A_smem[m][k_local];\n float a_f = __half2float(a_val);\n \n #pragma unroll\n for (int v = 0; v < 8; v++) {\n int n_local = n_thread_start + v;\n half b_val = B_dq_smem[k_local][n_local];\n acc[v] = fmaf(a_f, __half2float(b_val), acc[v]);\n }\n }\n }\n \n __syncthreads();\n }\n \n if (m < M) {\n #pragma unroll\n for (int v = 0; v < 8; v++) {\n int n = n_start + n_thread_start + v;\n if (n < N) {\n C[m * N + n] = __float2half(acc[v]);\n }\n }\n }\n}\n\ntorch::Tensor w4a16_gemm_h100(\n torch::Tensor A, torch::Tensor B_q, torch::Tensor scales, int64_t group_size\n) {\n int M = A.size(0);\n int K = A.size(1);\n int N = scales.size(1);\n \n auto C = torch::empty({M, N}, A.options());\n \n int blocks = (N + 127) / 128;\n \n w4a16_gemm_h100_kernel<<<blocks, 256>>>(\n reinterpret_cast<const half*>(A.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n B_q.data_ptr<int>(),\n reinterpret_cast<const half*>(scales.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n reinterpret_cast<half*>(C.data_ptr<at::Half>()),\n M, N, K, static_cast<int>(group_size)\n );\n \n return C;\n}\n'''\n\nw4a16_gemm_h100 = load_inline(\n name=\"w4a16_gemm_h100_v8\",\n cpp_sources=cpp_source,\n cuda_sources=cuda_source,\n functions=[\"w4a16_gemm_h100\"],\n extra_cuda_cflags=[\"-O3\", \"--use_fast_math\", \"-gencode=arch=compute_90,code=sm_90\"],\n extra_cflags=[\"-O3\"],\n verbose=False,\n)\n\nclass ModelNew(nn.Module):\n def __init__(self, K, N, GROUP_SIZE):\n super().__init__()\n self.GROUP_SIZE = GROUP_SIZE\n torch.manual_seed(0)\n w = torch.randn(K, N, dtype=torch.float16, device=\"cuda\")\n \n num_groups = K // GROUP_SIZE\n w_reshaped = w.view(num_groups, GROUP_SIZE, N)\n maxabs = w_reshaped.abs().amax(dim=1, keepdim=True).clamp(min=1e-6)\n scale = maxabs / 7.0\n \n w_int = torch.clamp(torch.round(w_reshaped / scale) + 8, 0, 15).to(torch.int32)\n w_int = w_int.view(K, N)\n \n b_q = torch.zeros(K // 16, 2 * N, dtype=torch.int32, device=\"cuda\")\n for k_in in range(8):\n b_q[:, 0::2] |= (w_int[k_in::16, :] << (k_in * 4))\n for k_in in range(8, 16):\n b_q[:, 1::2] |= (w_int[k_in::16, :] << ((k_in - 8) * 4))\n \n self.register_buffer(\"b_q_weight\", b_q.contiguous())\n self.register_buffer(\"b_scales\", scale.squeeze(1).half().contiguous())\n \n def forward(self, x):\n return w4a16_gemm_h100.w4a16_gemm_h100(x, self.b_q_weight, self.b_scales, self.GROUP_SIZE)\n" }
